The Index and Core of a Relation With Applications to the Axiomatics of Relation Algebra
Roland Backhouse, Ed Voermans

Abstract
We introduce the general notions of an index and a core of a relation. We postulate a limited form of the axiom of choice —specifically that all partial equivalence relations have an index— and explore the consequences of adding the axiom to standard axiom systems for point-free reasoning. Examples of the theorems we prove are that a core/index of a difunction is a bijection, and that the so-called “all or nothing” axiom used to facilitate pointwise reasoning is derivable from our axiom of choice.



1
Introduction

We introduce the general notions of an “index” and a “core” of a relation. As suggested by the terminology,
the practical significance of both notions is to substantially reduce the size of
a (possibly very large) binary relation in such a way that the relation can nevertheless easily be
recovered. Example
1
illustrates the notions.

Example 1

Fig.
1
depicts a relation (on the left) and two instances of cores of
the relation (in the middle and on the right). All are depicted as bipartite graphs. The relation
R
is a
relation on blue and red nodes. The middle figure depicts a core as a relation on squares of blue nodes
and squares of red nodes, each square being an equivalence class of the left per domain of
R
(on the left)
or of the right per domain of
R
(on the right). The rightmost figure depicts a core as a relation on
representatives of the equivalence classes: the relation depicted by the thick green edges. The rightmost
figure also depicts an index of the relation; the middle does not: although the relations depicted in the
middle and rightmost figures are isomorphic, they have different types.

Figure 1:
A Relation, a Core and an Index.

\Box

Although the notion of the “core” of a relation is more general than the notion of an “index”,
a significant disadvantage is that a “core” typically has a type that is different from the
relation itself; in contrast, an “index” of a relation is a “core” that has the same type as the relation.
This is useful for practical purposes, particularly in the context of heterogeneous relations, because it avoids the
necessity to introduce type judgements. For this reason, our focus us on the notion of an index.

The paper is divided into three parts. The first part, consisting of
sections
2
and
3
, sets up the framework on which our calculations are
based. Section
2
summarises the axiom system and the notation we use;
section
3
lists a number of derived concepts and their properties. These properties are given
without proof.

In section
4
, we formalise the notions of a “core” and an
“index” of a relation in the context of point-free relation algebra. We establish a large collection of
properties of these notions which form a basis for the third part of the paper. (Because the notions are
new, almost all the properties are new. An example of a property that some readers may recognise,
albeit expressed differently, is that a difunction has an index that is a bijection.)

Section
5
specialises the notion of an index to partial equivalence
relations and difunctions. Section
5.2
concludes by the introduction of a restricted form of the axiom of
choice: we postulate that every partial equivalence relation has an index. This is the same as saying that
it is possible to choose a representative element of every equivalence class of a partial equivalence
relation. Section
5.3
then shows that every relation has an index.
Section
6
is about applying our axiom of choice to the derivation of well-known
characterisations of pers and difunctions.

Section
7
examines the consequences of adding our axiom of choice to point-free
relation algebra in order to facilitate pointwise reasoning. We show that so doing has surprising and
remarkable consequences. One such consequence is that we can derive the so-called “all-or-nothing”
rule; this is a rule introduced by Glück
[
Glü17
]
also as a means of facilitating pointwise reasoning.
(See
[
BDGv22
]
for examples of how the rule is used in reasoning about graphs.) The
main theorem in section
7
is that, with the addition of our axiom of choice and an
extensionality axiom, the type

A{\sim}B
of relations is isomorphic to the powerset
2^{A{{\times}}B}
(the set of subsets of the
cartesian product of
A
and
B
). Section
8
presents several models of
point-free relation algebra clarifying the relation of the additional axioms to each other.

Section
9
concludes the paper with a discussion of the significance of the
notions we have introduced and a pointer to the potential value for practical applications.

2
Axioms of Point-free Relation Algebra

In traditional, pointwise reasoning about relations, it is not the relations themselves that are the focus of
interest. Rather, a relation
R
of type
A{\sim}B
is defined to be a subset of the cartesian product

A{{\times}}B
and the focus of interest is the boolean membership
property
(a,b){\in}R
where
a
and
b
are elements of type
A
and
B
, respectively. Equality of relations
R
and
S

is defined in terms of membership (typically in terms of “if and only if”), leading to a lack of
concision (and frequently precision). In point-free relation algebra, the membership relation plays no
role, and reasoning is truly about properties of relations.

In this section, we give a brief summary of the axioms of point-free relation algebra. For full details
of the axioms, see
[
BDGv22
]
. For a summary of our notational conventions, see the
appendix (section
9
).

2.1
Summary

Point-free relation algebra comprises three layers with interfaces between the layers plus additional axioms
peculiar to relations. The axiom system is typed. For types
A
and
B
,
A{\sim}B
denotes a set; the elements of the set are called

(heterogeneous) relations of type

A{\sim}B
. Elements of type
A{\sim}A
, for some type
A
, are called
homogeneous
relations
.

The first layer axiomatises the properties of a partially ordered set. We postulate that, for each pair of
types
A
and
B
,
A{\sim}B
forms a complete, universally distributive lattice. In anticipation of section

7
, where
we add axioms that require
A{\sim}B
to be a powerset, we use the symbol “
{\subseteq}
” for the ordering relation, and “
{\cup}
”
and “
{\cap}
” for the supremum and infimum operators. We assume that this notation is familiar to the
reader, allowing us to skip a more detailed account of its properties. However, we use
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

for the least element of the ordering (rather than the conventional
\emptyset
) and
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
for the greatest element. In keeping with the
conventional practice of overloading the symbol “
\emptyset
”, both these symbols are overloaded. The symbols
“
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
” and “
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
” are pronounced “bottom” and “top”, respectively. (Strictly we should write something like

{}_{A}{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}_{B}
and
{}_{A}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}_{B}
for the bottom and top elements of type
A{\sim}B
. Of course, care needs to be taken when
overloading operators in this way but it is usually the case that elementary type considerations allow the
appropriate type to be deduced.)

It is important to note that there is no axiom stating that a relation is a set, and there is no corresponding
notion of membership. (In, for example,
[
ABH+92
]
and
[
Voe99
]
, we used the symbols “
{\sqsubseteq}
”, “
{\sqcup}
” and “
{\sqcap}
”
and the name “spec calculus” rather than “relation algebra” in order to avoid
misunderstanding.) The lack of a notion of membership distinguishes point-free relation algebra from
pointwise algebra.

The second layer adds a composition operator. If
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
is a relation of type

B{\sim}C
, the composition of
R
and
S
is a relation of type
A{\sim}C
which we denote by
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
. Composition is
associative and, for each type
A
, there is an identity relation which we denote by
\mathbb{I}_{A}
. We often overload
the notation for the identity relation, writing just
\mathbb{I}
. Occasionally, for greater clarity, we do supply the
type information.

The interface between the first and second layers defines a relation algebra to be an instance of a
regular
algebra

[
Bac06
]
(also called a
standard Kleene algebra
, or
S
-
algebra

[
Con71
]
). For this paper,
the most important aspect of this interface is the existence and properties of the factor operators. These
are introduced in section
2.2
. Also,
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
is a zero of composition: for all
R
,

{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
.

The completeness axiom in the first layer allows the reflexive-transitive closure
R^{*}
of
each element
R
of type
A{\sim}A
, for some type
A
, to be defined. For practical applications, this is possibly the
most important aspect of regular algebra but such applications are not considered in this paper. For this
paper, completeness is only relevant when we add axioms to the algebra that model pointwise reasoning.
We do require, however, the existence of
R{\cup}S
and
R{\cap}S
, for all pairs of relations
R
and
S
of the same
type, and the usual properties of set union and intersection.

The third layer is the introduction of a
converse
operator. If
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
, the converse of

R
, which we denote by
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
(pronounced
R
“wok”) is a relation of type
B{\sim}A
.
The interface with the first layer is that converse is a poset isomorphism (in particular,
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
and

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
), and the interface with the
second layer is formed by the two rules
\mathbb{I}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
and, for all relations
R
and
S
of appropriate type,

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
.

Additional axioms characterise properties peculiar to relations.
The modularity rule (aka Dedekind’s rule
[
Rig48
]
) is that, for all relations
R
,
S
and
T
,

(2)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(S\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT)~~.

The dual property, obtained by exploiting properties of the converse operator, is, for all relations
R
,
S
and
T
,

(3)

S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(S\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

The modularity rule is
necessary to the derivation of some of the properties we state without proof (for example, the
properties of the domain operators given in section
3.1
). Another rule is the
cone rule
:

(4)

{\left\langle\forall{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{:}{:}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 4.49982pt{\vee}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\right\rangle}~~.

The cone rule limits consideration to “unary” relation algebras: constructing the cartesian product of two
relation algebras to form a relation algebra (whereby the operators are defined pointwise) does not yield
an algebra satisfying the cone rule.

2.2
Factors

If
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
is a relation of type
A{\sim}C
, the relation
R{\setminus}S
of type
B{\sim}C
is defined by
the Galois connection, for all
T
(of type
B{\sim}C
),

(5)

T\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\setminus}S\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptS~~.

Similarly, if
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
is a relation of type
C{\sim}B
, the relation
R{/}S
of type
A{\sim}C
is defined by
the Galois connection, for all
T
,

(6)

T\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{/}S\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR~~.

The existence of factors is a property of a regular algebra; in relation algebra, factors are also known as
“residuals”. Factors have the
cancellation
properties:

(7)

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\setminus}U\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptU\hskip 13.49945pt{\wedge}\hskip 13.49945ptR{/}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR~~.

The relations
R{\setminus}R
(of type
B{\sim}B
if
R
has type
A{\sim}B
) and
R{/}R
(of type
A{\sim}A
if
R
has type
A{\sim}B
) play a
central role in what follows. As is easily verified, both are
preorders
. That is, both are
transitive
:

(8)

R{\setminus}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 13.49945pt{\wedge}\hskip 13.49945ptR{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{/}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R

and both are
reflexive
:

(9)

\mathbb{I}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951pt\mathbb{I}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{/}R~~.

(The notation “
\mathbb{I}
” is overloaded in the above equation. In the left conjunct, it
denotes the identity relation of type
B{\sim}B
and, in the right conjunct, it
denotes the identity relation of type
A{\sim}A
, assuming
R
has type
A{\sim}B
.) We also have the cancellation
property, for all
R
,

(10)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptR\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptR{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Factors enjoy a rich theory which underlies many of our calculations. However, for space reasons, we
omit further details here.

3
Some Definitions

In point-free relation algebra, “coreflexives” of a given type represent sets of elements of that type.
A
coreflexive of type

A
is a relation
p
such that
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}_{A}
. Frequently used properties are that, for all
coreflexives
p
,

p\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p

and, for all coreflexives
p
and
q
,

p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}q\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptq\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptq{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p~~.

(The proof of these properties relies on the modularity rule.) In the literature, coreflexives have several different names, usually depending on the application area in
question. Examples are “monotype”, “pid” (short for “partial identity”) and “test”.

3.1
The Domain Operators

The “domain operators” (see eg.
[
BH93
]
) play a dominant and unavoidable role. We
exploit their properties frequently in calculations, so much so that we assume great familiarity with
them.

Definition 11
(Domain Operators)

Given relation
R
of type
A{\sim}B
,
the
left domain

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
of
R
is a relation of type
A
defined by the equation

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963pt\mathbb{I}_{A}\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

and the
right domain

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
of
R
is a relation of type
B
is defined by the equation

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963pt\mathbb{I}_{B}\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

\Box

The name “domain operator” is chosen because of the fundamental properties: for all
R
and all
coreflexives
p
,

(12)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptp

and

(13)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

A simple, often-used consequence of (
12
) and (
13
) is the property:

(14)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

In words,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
is the least coreflexive
p
such that restricting the “domain” of
R
on the right has no effect on

R
. It is in this sense that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
represent the set of points on the left and on the right on which the
relation
R
is “defined”, i.e. its left and right “domains”.

By instantiating
p
to
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
in (
12
) and (
13
) we get

(15)

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot})\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot})\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot})~~.

Additional properties used frequently below are as follows.

Theorem 16

For all relations
R
and coreflexives
p
,

(17)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p\mbox{~~and~~}R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\mbox{~~,}

(18)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p\mbox{~~and~~}R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R~~.

\Box

Theorem 19

For all relations
R
and
S
,

(a)

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
,

(b)

(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
, and

(c)

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
and
(R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
.

\Box

We also use the fact that the domain operators are monotonic (as is evident from definition
11
).

3.2
Pers and Per Domains

Given relations
R
of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
of type
A{\sim}C
, the symmetric

right-division
is the relation
R{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}S
of type
B{\sim}C
defined in terms of
right
factors as

(20)

R{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}S\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\setminus}S\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt(S{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Dually, given relations
R
of type
B{\sim}A
and
S
of type
C{\sim}A
, the
symmetric
left-division
is the relation
R/{\hskip-3.41432pt}/{}S
of type
B{\sim}C
defined in terms of left factors as

(21)

R/{\hskip-3.41432pt}/{}S\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{/}S\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982pt(S{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

The relation
R{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R
is an equivalence relation
1
1

1

This is a well-known fact: the relation
R{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R
is the
symmetric closure of the preorder
R{\setminus}R
. The easy proof is left to the reader.
.
Voermans
[
Voe99
]
calls it the “greatest
right domain” of
R
. Riguet
[
Rig48
]
calls
R{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R
the “noyau” of
R
(but defines it using nested
complements). Others (see
[
Oli18
]
for references) call it the “kernel” of
R
.

As remarked elsewhere
[
Oli18
]
, the
symmetric left-division
inherits a number of (left) cancellation
properties from the properties of factorisation in terms of which it is defined. For our purposes, the only
cancellation property we use is the following (inherited from (
10
)). For all
R
,

(22)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR/{\hskip-3.41432pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

In this section the focus is on the left and right
“per domains” introduced by Voermans
[
Voe99
]
.

Definition 23
(Right and Left Per Domains)

The
right per domain
of relation
R
, denoted

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
, is defined by the equation

(24)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R~~.

Dually, the
left per domain
of relation
R
, denoted
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
, is defined by the equation

(25)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR/{\hskip-3.41432pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

\Box

The left and right per domains are “pers” where “per” is an abbreviation of “partial equivalence
relation”.

Definition 26
(Partial Equivalence Relation (per))

A relation is a
partial equivalence
relation
iff it is symmetric and transitive. That is,
R
is a partial equivalence relation iff

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Henceforth we abbreviate partial equivalence relation to
per
.

\Box

That
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
are indeed pers is a direct consequence of the symmetry and transitivity of
R{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R
.

The left and right per domains are called “domains” because, like the coreflexive domains, we have the
properties: for all relations
R
and pers
P
,

(27)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptP\mbox{~~, and}

(28)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptP{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptP\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}~~.

As with the coreflexive domains, we also have:

(29)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

(The second of these equalities is an immediate consequence of (
22
) and the
properties of (coreflexive-) domains; the first is symmetric.)
Indeed,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
are the “least” pers that satisfy the equalities (
29
). See

[
Voe99
]
for details of the ordering relation on pers.

The right per domain
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
can be defined equivalently by the equation

(30)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-3.41432pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Moreover,

(31)

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

(See
[
Bac21
]
for the proofs of these properties.) Symmetrical properties hold of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
.

The following lemma extends
[
Rig48
, Corollaire, p.134]
from equivalence relations to pers.

Lemma 32

For all relations
R
, the following statements are all equivalent.

(i)

R
is a per (i.e.
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
) ,

(ii)

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(iii)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
,

(iv)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
.

\Box

For further properties of pers and per domains, see
[
Voe99
]
.

3.3
Functionality

A relation
R
of type
A{\sim}B

is said to be
left
-
functional
iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
. Equivalently,
R
is
left-functional
iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathbb{I}_{A}
.
It is said to be
right-functional
iff
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
(equivalently,
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathbb{I}_{B}
). A relation
R

is said to be a
bijection
iff it is both left- and right-functional.

Rather than left- and right-functional, the more common terminology is “functional” and “injective”
but publications differ on which of left- or right-functional is “functional” or “injective”. We choose to
abbreviate “left-functional” to
functional
and to use the term
injective
instead of right-functional.
Typically, we use
f
and
g
to denote functional relations, and Greek letters to denote bijections (although
the latter is not always the case). Other authors make the opposite choice.

3.4
Difunctions

Formally, relation
R
is
difunctional
iff

(33)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~.

As for pers, there are several equivalent definitions of “difunctional”. We begin with the point-free
definitions:

Theorem 34

For all
R
, the following statements are all equivalent.

(i)

R
is difunctional (i.e.
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR
) ,

(ii)

R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(iii)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(iv)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(v)

R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
,

(vi)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
,

(vii)

R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
.

\Box

The equivalence of
34
(i) and
34
(ii) is well-known and due to
Riguet
[
Rig48
,
Rig50
]
; the equivalence of
34
(i), (iv) and (vi) is due to Voermans

[
Voe99
]
. The equivalence of
34
(i), (iii) and (v) is formally stronger: a
consequence is that, if
R
is difunctional,

(35)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

(Cf. (
24
).)

Riguet
[
Rig51
]
calls the right side of
34
(vii) the “différence” of relation
R
,
although he defines it using nested complements rather than factors. The name “différence” is
motivated by his definition. Since we avoid the use of complements (and for other reasons) we prefer
the term “diagonal”; see
[
BV22
,
BV23
]
for further discussion.

The equivalence of (i) and (vii) is
a straightforward but beautiful application of the Galois connections defining intersection, converse and
factors.

Definition (
33
) is the most useful when it is required to establish that a
particular relation is difunctional, whereas properties
34
(ii)-(vii) are more useful
when it is required to exploit the fact that a particular relation is difunctional.

The combination of theorem
34
(in particular
34
(ii) and

34
(iv) with lemma
32
allows one to prove that a per is a symmetric
difunction. (We leave the easy calculation to the reader.) This property is sometimes used to specialise
properties of difunctions to properties of pers.

3.5
Squares and Rectangles

We now introduce the notions of a “rectangle” and a “square”; rectangles are typically heterogeneous
whilst squares are, by definition, homogeneous relations. Squares are rectangles; properties of squares
are typically obtained by specialising properties of rectangles. (Riguet
[
Rig48
]
uses the terms
“rectangle” and “carré”.)

Definition 36
(Rectangle and Square)

A relation
R
is a
rectangle
iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
. A relation
R

is a
square
iff
R
is a symmetric rectangle.

\Box

It is easily shown that a rectangle is a difunction and a square is a per.

Lemma 37

For all relations
R
and
S
,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
is a rectangle.
It follows that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
is a rectangle if
T
is a rectangle.

Proof
Because the proof is based on the cone rule, a case analysis is necessary. In the case that either
R

or
S
is the empty relation, the lemma clearly holds (because
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
is the empty relation, and the empty
relation is a rectangle). Suppose now that both
R
and
S
are non-empty. Then

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S

=

\{

cone rule: (
4
) (applied twice), assumption:
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
and
S\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S~~.

If
T
is a rectangle,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
; thus
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
is a rectangle.

\Box

3.6
Isomorphic Relations

The (yet-to-be-defined) cores and indexes of a given relation are not unique; in common mathematical
jargon, they are unique “up to isomorphism”. In order to make this precise, we need to define the
notion of isomorphic relation and establish a number of properties.

Definition 38

Suppose
R
and
S
are two relations (not necessarily of the same type). Then we say
that
R
and
S
are
isomorphic
and write
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
iff

\langle\exists\hskip 1.49994pt\phi{,}\psi

:

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

:

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\rangle
~~.

\Box

The relation between
R
and
S
in definition
38
can be strengthened to the conjunction

(39)

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS~~.

Alternatively, the leftmost conjunct can be replaced by the rightmost conjunct. This is a consequence of
the following lemma.

Lemma 40

For all
\phi
,
\psi
,
R
and
S
,

(R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS)

\Leftarrow

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

\Box

We often choose one or other of the conjuncts in (
39
), whichever being most convenient at the
time.

Lemma 41

The relation
{\cong}
is reflexive, transitive and symmetric. That is,
{\cong}
is an equivalence
relation.

\Box

The task of proving that two relations are isomorphic involves constructing
\phi
and
\psi
that satisfy the
conditions of the existential quantification in definition
38
; we call the constructed values

witnesses
to the isomorphism.

Note that the requirement on
\phi
in definition
38
is that it is both functional and injective; thus it
is required to “witness” a (1–1) correspondence between the points in the left domain of
R
and the points
in the left domain of
S
. Similarly, the requirement on
\psi
is that it “witnesses” a (1–1) correspondence
between the points in the right domain of
R
and the points in the right domain of
S
. Formally,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and

S{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
are isomorphic as “witnessed” by
\phi
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
and
S{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are isomorphic as “witnessed” by
\psi
:

Lemma 42

Suppose
R
and
S
are relations such that
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
. Then
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.
Specifically, if
\phi
and
\psi
witness the isomorphism
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
,

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Proof
Suppose
\phi
and
\psi
are such that

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Then

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
is a coreflexive
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

assumption
~~~\}

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

assumption
~~~\}

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

That is
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. Similarly,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. But also (because the domain operators are closure
operators),

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(S{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(S{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Applying definition
38
with
R{,}S{,}\phi{,}\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi
and
R{,}S{,}\phi{,}\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi
, the lemma is proved.

\Box

The property of the left and right domains stated in lemma
42
is also valid for the left and
right per domains:

Lemma 43

Suppose
R
and
S
are relations such that
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
. Then
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
.
Specifically, if
\phi
and
\psi
witness the isomorphism
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
,

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Proof
Suppose
\phi
and
\psi
witness the isomorphism
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
. We show that the pair
(\psi,\psi)
witnesses the
isomorphism
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
. By assumption,
\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
,
\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
. Moreover, for all

R
,
(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
; thus

\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
and
\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(S{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
. So it remains to show that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. Now

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

transitivity
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

The calculation thus splits into two steps: the proof of the leftmost equality and the proof of the rightmost
equality. The leftmost equality proceeds as follows.

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

(
27
),
\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is a per (see below)
~~~\}

R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Continuing with the right hand side:

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
~~~\}

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
, domains: (
14
) and (
29
)
~~~\}

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

see lemma
41
~~~\}

R~~.

Combining the two calculations, we have established that

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Now, for the rightmost equality, we have:

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
, domains: (
14
)
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
~~~\}

\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

Leibniz
~~~\}

\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

=

\{

converse (noting that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
and
S{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
are symmetric)
~~~\}

=

\{

(
27
),
\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi
is a per (see below)
~~~\}

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptS

\{

as above, with
