R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

=

\{

49
(b)
~~~\}

R~~.

\Box

The property that
R
is a difunction is equivalent to
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
(and symmetrically to
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
).
Also, since
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
, the right side of lemma
55
simplifies to
R
. In this way, the
definition of an index of a difunction can be restated as follows.

Definition 69
(Difunction Index)

An index of a difunction
R
is a relation
J
that has the following properties:

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~,

(b)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~.

(c)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(d)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

\Box

Lemma 70

An index
J
of a difunction
R
is a bijection between
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.

Proof

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

69
(c)
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

\supseteq

\{

69
(a)
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

domains: (
14
) and theorem
19
(b)
~~~\}

J^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ

\supseteq

\{

domains: definition
11
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Thus, by anti-symmetry,

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ~~.

Symmetrically,
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. That is,
J
is a bijection.

\Box

Corollary
71
formulates a method to determine whether a relation is a
difunction: compute an index of the relation and then determine whether it is a difunction. By

49
(a), the second step in this process will be no less efficient than determining difunctionality
directly and, in many cases, may be substantially more efficient. (There is, however, no guarantee of
improved efficiency since the inequality in
49
(a) may be an equality.)

Corollary 71

Suppose
J
is an index of relation
R
. Then
R
is a difunction iff
J
is a
difunction.

Proof
Lemma
68
establishes “if”. Lemma
70
establishes “only
if” (since a bijection is a difunction).
\Box

5.2
Indexes of Pers

That every difunction has an index is a desirable property but it is not provable in standard
axiomatic formulations of relation algebra. Rather than postulate its truth, we shall postulate that all
pers have an index, and then show that a consequence of the postulate is that all difunctions have an
index.

A relation
R
is a per iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
. Using this property, the definition of index can be simplified for pers.
Specifically, an index
J
of per
R
has the following properties. (Cf. definition
49
.)

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~,

(b)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR~~,

(c)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(d)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Lemmas
72
and
73
prepare the way for definition
74
.

Lemma 72

If a per has an index, then it has an index that is a coreflexive.

Proof
Suppose
R
is a per and
J
is an index of
R
.
The lemma is proved if we show that
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
is an index of
R
. We thus have to prove that

(e)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~,

(f)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~,

(g)

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(h)

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

assuming the properties (a), (b), (c) and (d) above.

Of the four properties, only (f) is non-trivial. (Properties (g) and (h) follow because
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
. Property (e) follows because, since
R
is a per,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
.)

Property (f) is proved as follows.

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

by lemma
70
,
J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

domains: (
14
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

(d)
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

domains: (
14
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

(b)
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

R
is a per, so
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
; converse
~~~\}

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

R
is a per, so
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
; (b) and converse
~~~\}

R~~.

\Box

Lemma 73

For all pers
R
, if
R
has an index then there is a relation
J
such that

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(b)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptJ~~,

(c)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR~~.

Conversely, for all pers
R
, if relation
J
satisfies the properties (a), (b) and (c) above, then
J
is an index of

R
.

Proof
First, suppose
R
is a per that has an index. By lemma
72
,
R
has a coreflexive index.
Let
J
be such a coreflexive index of
R
. We must show that properties (a), (b) and (c) hold.
We have

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

49
(a) and monotonicity
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

J
is a coreflexive
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

This proves (a). Now for (b):

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J

=

\{

J
is a coreflexive, so
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
,

R
is a per, so
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

49
(c)
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

J
is a coreflexive, so
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

J~~.

Finally, (c):

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R

=

\{

R
is a per, so
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}

=

\{

49
(b)
~~~\}

R~~.

For the converse, assume
R
is a per and
J
satisifies the properties (a), (b) and (c) above. We have to check
the four properties listed in definition
49
. First,
49
(a):

J

\subseteq

\{

assumption: (a) above
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

\subseteq

\{

R
is per
~~~\}

R~~.

The properties
49
(b), (c) and (d) follow because
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
and
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
.

\Box

As a consequence of lemma
73
, we postulate the following definition of an index of a per.

Definition 74
(Index of a Per)

Suppose
P
is a per. Then a (
coreflexive
)

index
of
P
is a relation
J
such that

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptP{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(b)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptJ~~,

(c)

P{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptP~~.

\Box

We also postulate that every per has a coreflexive index. We call this the
axiom of choice
.

Axiom 75
(Axiom of Choice)

Every per has a coreflexive index.

\Box

5.3
From Pers To Relations

It is a desirable property that every relation has an index. However, as mentioned earlier, this can’t be
proved in standard relation algebra. It can be proved if we assume that every per has an index. The
construction is suggested by theorem
59
.

Theorem 76

Suppose
J
and
K
are (coreflexive) indices of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
, respectively. Then
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K
is an index of
R
.

Proof
For convenience, we list the properties of
J
and
K.
These are obtained by instantiating definition

74
with
J{,}R\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
J{,}R\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
. (Domain properties have been used to simplify
(a) and (d).)

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(b)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ~~,

(c)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}~~,

(d)

K\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

(e)

K\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptK~~,

(f)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

We have to prove the four properties
49
(a)-(d) with the instantiation
J{,}R\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptR
.
By (a),
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
. Similarly for
K
. The proof obligations are thus:

(g)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR~~,

(h)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~.

(i)

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(j)

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

Property (g) is an easy combination of (a) and (d). For (h) we have:

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

=

\{

per domains: (
29
)
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

=

\{

(b) and (f)
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

=

\{

per domains: (
29
)
~~~\}

R~~.

For (i), we have

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptK{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptK
,

composition of coreflexives is intersection
~~~\}

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

(e)
~~~\}

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptK\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptK{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptK

composition of coreflexives is intersection
~~~\}

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

The proof is (j) is symmetrical.

\Box

Theorem
76
shows how to construct an index of a relation
R
from indexes
J
and
K
of its left
and right per domains. In combination with lemma
53
and corollary
54
, the
construction is unique. Specifically, the steps are, first to choose from each equivalence class of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
each equivalence class of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
a single representative. The collection of such representatives defines the
coreflexives
J
and
K
. Then the index is defined to be
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K
.

6
Characterisations of Pers and Difunctions

This section is about characterising pers and difunctions in terms of functional relations. Although the
characterisations are well known, they are not derivable in point-free relation algebra. We show that
they are derivable using our axiom of choice.

6.1
Characterisation of Pers

A well-known property is that a relation
R
is a per iff

(77)

{\left\langle\exists{}f\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\right\rangle}~~.

This property is said to be a
characteristic
property of pers. Perhaps surprisingly, it is
not
derivable in
systems that axiomatise point-free relation algebra. Freyd and Ščedrov
[
Fv90
, 1.281]
call
the functional
f
witnessing the existential quantification a ‘‘splitting
2
2

2

Freyd and
Ščedrov define a “splitting” in the more general context of a category rather than an allegory;
the notion is applicable to “idempotents” which are also more general than pers.
” of
R.
Typically, the
existence of “splittings” is either postulated as an axiom (eg. Winter
[
Win04
]
) or by adding
axioms formulating relations as a so-called “power allegory”
[
Fv90
, 2.422]
, or by adding the
so-called “all-or-nothing” axiom
[
Glü17
]
. (See section
7.6
for discussion of “all or
nothing”.) See
[
BO23
]
for a comparison of the techniques used to establish (
77
) using
these different axiom systems. Here we show that the existence of “splittings” is a consequence of our
axiom of choice:

Theorem 78

If per
P
has a coreflexive index
J
, then

P\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951ptJ\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Thus, assuming the axiom of choice, for all relations
R
,

\mathsf{per}{.}R\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997pt{\left\langle\exists{}f\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\right\rangle}~~.

Proof
The proof is very straightforward. We have

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)

=

\{

distributivity
~~~\}

P^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptP

=

\{

J
is coreflexive, so
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ
;
P\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptP^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
~~~\}

P{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P

=

\{

J
is an index of
P
, definition
74
(c)
~~~\}

P

and

(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

distributivity
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptP\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptP^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ

=

\{

P
is a per, so by lemma
32
(ii),
P\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptP^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptP
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J

=

\{

J
is an index of
P
, definition
74
(b)
~~~\}

J~~.

This proves the first property. It also establishes that (assuming the axiom of choice), for all
R
,

\mathsf{per}{.}R\hskip 7.4997pt{\Rightarrow}\hskip 7.4997pt{\left\langle\exists{}f\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\right\rangle}~~.

(The witness is
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
.) The converse is obvious: see
[
BO23
]
for details.
The equivalence follows by mutual implication.

\Box

A second so-called “characteristic” property is that a relation
R
is a difunctional iff

{\left\langle\exists\hskip 1.49994pt{}f{,}g\hskip 8.99963pt{:}\hskip 8.99963ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{:}\hskip 8.99963ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\right\rangle}~~.

Like the characteristic property of pers, it is not derivable in systems that axiomatise point-free relation
algebra. It is, however, a corollary of the existence of “splittings” (and thus of theorem
78
),
as shown by Winter
[
Win04
]
.

6.2
Unicity of Characterisations

The characterisation of a per in the form
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
where
f
is a functional relation is not unique. (There are
typically many representatives one can choose for each equivalence class; so there are very many
distinct indexes of a per.) The characterisation is sometimes described as being “essentially” unique or
sometimes as unique “up to isomorphism”. See our working document
[
BV22
]
for full details.

7
Enabling Pointwise Reasoning

In this section, our goal is to capture the notion that a relation is a set with elements pairs of points.

In traditional pointwise reasoning about relations, a basic assumption is that a type is a set that forms a
complete, universally distributive lattice under the subset ordering; the type of a (binary) relation is a set
of pairs. The set of relations of a given type thus forms a powerset of a set of pairs.

In section
7.1
, we recall a general theorem on the structure of powersets. Briefly,
theorem
81
states that a set is isomorphic to the powerset of its “atoms” iff it is
“saturated”. The section defines these concepts; the concepts then form the backbone of later sections
where we specialise the theorem to relations.

One (of several) mechanisms for introducing pointwise reasoning within the framework of point-free
relation algebra involves the introduction of the so-called “all-or-nothing rule” which was postulated as
an axiom by Glück
[
Glü17
]
. This rule is combined with completeness and “extensionality” axioms
which state that, for each type
A
, the coreflexives of type
A
form a complete, saturated lattice. This was the
approach taken in
[
BDGv22
]
where pointwise reasoning was used to
formulate and prove properties of graphs. Theorem
102
establishes that the
all-or-nothing rule is a consequence of our axiom of choice (axiom
75
:
every per has an index). Together with the “extensionality” axiom, this enables the application of
theorem
81
to establish that the type
A{\sim}B
is isomorphic to the powerset
2^{A{{\times}}B}
(the set
of subsets of the cartesian product
A{{\times}}B
). See theorems
102
and
103
in section

7.6
.

Section
7.2
introduces “points” and states the extensionality axiom
that we assume. A number of sections are then necessary in order to establish theorem
103
.
Section
7.3
introduces “particles” and “pairs”; it is then shown that particles are points whilst section

7.4
shows that —assuming the axiom of choice— points are particles. (For
this reason, the terminology “particle” is temporary.) Section
7.5
shows that proper
atoms (of a given type) are “pairs”. These are the ingredients for deriving the “all-or-nothing” rule in
section
7.6
. Section
7.6
also shows that the point-free definition of a “pair” in
section
7.3
does correspond to what one normally understands to be a pair of points. The section
concludes with theorem
103
.

7.1
Powersets

As mentioned above, this section defines “atoms” and “saturated” in the context of a partially ordered
set. We then state a fundamental theorem relating these concepts to powersets.

The definition of an atom is the following.

Definition 79
(Atom and Atomicity)

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a set partially ordered by the relation

{\sqsubseteq}
. Then, the element
p
is an
atom
iff

{\left\langle\forall{}q\hskip 5.99976pt{:}{:}\hskip 5.99976ptq\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptq\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 4.49982pt{\vee}\hskip 4.49982ptq\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\right\rangle}~~.

Note that
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
is an atom according to this definition. If
p
is an atom that is different from
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

we say that it is a
proper
atom. A lattice is said to be
atomic
if

{\left\langle\forall{}q\hskip 5.99976pt{:}{:}\hskip 5.99976ptq\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976pt{\left\langle\exists{}a\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{atom}{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\wedge}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptq\right\rangle}\right\rangle}~~.

In words, a lattice is atomic if every proper element includes a proper atom.

\Box

The definition of saturated is as follows.

Definition 80
(Saturated)

A complete lattice (ordered by
{\sqsubseteq}
) is
saturated
iff

{\left\langle\forall{}p\hskip 4.49982pt{:}{:}\hskip 4.49982ptp\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\left\langle\sqcup{}a\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{atom}{.}a\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta\right\rangle}\right\rangle}~~.

\Box

The set of subsets of a type is a
powerset iff the lattice is saturated, as formulated in the following theorem.

Theorem 81

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a complete, universally distributive lattice. Then the
following statements are equivalent.

(a)

\mathcal{A}
is saturated,

(b)

\mathcal{A}
is atomic and complemented,

(c)

\mathcal{A}
is isomorphic to the powerset of its atoms.

\Box

(See
[
ABH+92
, theorem 6.43]
for the proof of theorem
81
.)

We use theorem
81
in two ways. Firstly, for all types
A
, we simply postulate that the
set of coreflexives of type
A
is isomorphic to a powerset under the
{\subseteq}
ordering: the atoms are the “points”
introduced in section
7.2
. Second, we use this postulate together with our
axiom of choice to show that, for all types
A
and
B
, the type
A{\sim}B
of (heterogeneous) relations is also
isomorphic to a powerset under the
{\subseteq}
ordering: the atoms are “pairs” introduced in section
7.3
. The
proof that “pairs” are indeed atoms is the subject of section
7.5
. A prelude to this is
theorem
94
, proved in sections
7.3
and
7.4
, which
asserts that “points” are a special case of “pairs”.

7.2
Points

We begin by postulating that each type
A
is a set of “points”. We also postulate that
the set of coreflexives of type
A
forms a complete, universally distributive lattice under the subset
ordering. Finally, we postulate that the lattice is saturated.
With theorem
81
in mind, we define “points” to be the proper atoms of the lattice:

Definition 82
(Point)

A homogeneous relation
a
of type
A
is a
point
iff it has the following
three properties.

(a)

a\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
,

(b)

a\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
, and

(c)

{\left\langle\forall{}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta\right\rangle}
.

In words, a point is a proper, coreflexive atom.

\Box

If
A
is a type, we use
a
,
a^{\prime}
etc. to denote points of type
A
. Similarly for points of type
B
. Points
represent elements of the appropriate type.

For points
a
and
a^{\prime}
of the same type,

(83)

a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta^{\prime}\hskip 5.99976pt{\vee}\hskip 5.99976pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~.

The proof is straightforward. Suppose
a
and
a^{\prime}
are points. Then

a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}

\Leftarrow

\{

a
is an atom, definition
79
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta

\Leftarrow

\{

a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~.

Interchanging
a
and
a^{\prime}
,

a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 2.99988pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 2.99988pta^{\prime}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~.

But, since composition of coreflexives is symmetric,
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta^{\prime}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
. We conclude that

a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta^{\prime}\hskip 7.4997pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 7.4997pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~.

This is equivalent to (
83
).

In point-free relation algebra, subsets of a type
are modelled by coreflexives of that type. In order to model the property that the coreflexives of a given
type form a lattice that is isomorphic to the set of subsets of the type we need to add to our
axiom system a
saturation
property, viz.:

Definition 84
(Saturation)

Suppose
A
is a type.
The lattice of coreflexives of type
A
is said to be
saturated
iff

(85)

{\left\langle\forall{}p\hskip 5.99976pt{:}{:}\hskip 5.99976ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}_{A}\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptp\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\left\langle\cup{}a\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pta\right\rangle}\right\rangle}~~.

\Box

The axiom that we call “extensionality” is then:

Axiom 86
(Extensionality)

For each type
A
, the points of type
A
form a
complete, universally distributive, saturated lattice under the subset ordering.

\Box

Applying theorem
81
, a consequence of axiom

86
is that the coreflexives of type
A
form a lattice that is
isomorphic to the powerset
2^{A}
. In this sense, the coreflexives in point-free relation algebra represent sets
of points in traditional pointwise formulations of relation algebra.

We now want to show how to formulate the property that the set of relations of type
A{\sim}B
is isomorphic to
the powerset
2^{A{{\times}}B}
, i.e. relations in point-free relation algebra represent pairs
(a,b)
of points
a
and
b
of
type
A
and
B
, respectively.

7.3
Pairs and Particles

We now turn our attention to the lattice of relations of a given type.
We begin with a point-free definition of a “pair”. In subsection
7.6
, we show
that definition
87
does indeed capture the notion of a “pair of points” whereby the points are the
“particles” also introduced in the definition.

Definition 87
(Pair)

A relation
Z
is a
pair
iff it has the following properties:

(a)

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~,

(b)

Z\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z~~,

(c)

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~,

(d)

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ~~.

We call a relation a
particle
if it is a pair and it is symmetric.

\Box

In words, a pair
Z
is a non-empty rectangle (properties
87
(a) and
87
(b))
that is a bijection on its left domain and right domains (properties
87
(c) and
87
(d)).

(Definition
87
was introduced in
[
Voe99
]
but using the terminology “singleton” instead of “pair”,
and “singleton square” instead of “particle”.)

Our goal is to prove that the points are exactly the particles. This section is about showing that a
particle is a point. See corollary
92
.

One task is to show that particles are atoms. The more general property, which we need in later sections,
is that pairs are atoms.

Lemma 88

A pair is an atom.

Proof
Suppose
Z
is a pair and suppose
Y
is such that
Y\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptZ
. By the definition of atom, definition
79
,
we must
show that
Y\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\vee}\hskip 2.99988ptY\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ
. Equivalently, assuming
Y\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
, we must show that
Y\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ
. This is done as follows.

Y

=

\{

assumption:
Y\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptZ
. So,
Y{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
Y{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
; domains: (
18
)
~~~\}

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptY\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

Z
is a pair, so
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(Z{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
; similarly for
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
~~~\}

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptY\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ

=

\{

domains: theorem
19
(a) and theorem
19
(b)
~~~\}

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptY\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ

=

\{

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptY\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptY
(see first step above)
~~~\}

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Y{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z

=

\{

assumption:
Y\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
, cone rule
~~~\}

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z

=

\{

Z
is a pair
~~~\}

Z~~.

\Box

Since a particle is, by definition, a pair, we have:

Corollary 89

A particle is an atom.

\Box

Lemma 90

A particle is coreflexive.

Proof
Suppose
Z
is square and a pair. Then

Z

=

\{

assumption:
Z
is a pair, so
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z
;

\left[\hskip 4.49982pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ\hskip 4.49982pt\right]
~~~\}

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ

=

\{

assumption:
Z
is a square, so
Z\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

~~~\}

Z^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ

=

\{

assumption:
Z
is a pair, so
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ
~~~\}

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

That is,
Z
equals
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
which is coreflexive.

\Box

Corollary 91
(Particle)

A relation
Z
is a
particle
iff it has the following three
properties.

(a)

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
,

(b)

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
, and

(c)

Z\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z
.

In words, a particle is a proper, coreflexive rectangle.

Proof
“Only-if” is the combination of the definition of a particle and lemma

90
. “If” is a straightforward consequence of the properties of domains and
coreflexives.
\Box

Corollary 92

A particle is a proper, coreflexive atom.
That is, a particle is a point.

Proof
This is a combination of lemmas
88
and
90
.
\Box

7.4
Points are Particles

We now prove the converse of corollary
92
. We use the assumption that every per
has a coreflexive index: the axiom of choice (axiom
75
).

Lemma 93

Assuming axiom
75
, a point is a particle.

Proof
Suppose that
a
is a point. Comparing the definition of a point, definition
82
, with the defining
properties of a particle, corollary
92
, it suffices to prove that
a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
.
Clearly
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
is a per. (The simple proof uses the fact that
a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
, because
a
is coreflexive, and

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
because
a\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
.) So, by the axiom of choice,
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
has an index
J
, say. We show that
J
is a
particle and
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta
.

To show that
J
is a particle, we must establish the three properties listed in
corollary
91
with the instantiation
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ
. Part (a) is proved as follows.

J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

\Rightarrow

\{

{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
is zero of composition
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

J
is an index of per
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
, definition
74
(c)
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

\Rightarrow

\{

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
and
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta
(because
a\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
)
~~~\}

a\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

\left[\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt\right]
with
R\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994pta
~~~\}

a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

assumption:
a
is proper, i.e.
a\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
~~~\}

\mathsf{false}~~.

We conclude that
J\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
. The next step is to show that
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta
.

J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta

\Leftarrow

\{

assumption:
a
is an atom
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 4.49982pt{\vee}\hskip 4.49982ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta

=

\{

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
(see above)
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta

=

\{

assumption:
a\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
, so
a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

J\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

assumption:
J
is an index of
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a

definition
74
(a)
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

Property (b) of corollary
91
immediately follows because
a
is coreflexive.
We now show that
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J
.

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J

=

\{

J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta
(proved above) and
a\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J

=

\{

assumption:
J
is an index of
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a

definition
74
(c) with
P\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
~~~\}

J~~.

We conclude that
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J
. Thus
a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
as required.

\Box

Combining corollary
92
with lemma
93
, we conclude:

Theorem 94

Assuming axiom
75
, a relation is a point iff it is a
particle.

\Box

7.5
Proper Atoms are Pairs

The goal of this section is to show that a proper atom is a pair. Aiming to exploit the equivalence of points
and particles, we begin with lemmas on the left and right domains of a proper atom.

Lemma 95

Suppose
R
is a proper atom. Then
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are proper
atoms
3
3

3

Note: strictly we should detail the lattice under consideration here. However, it is easy to
show that a coreflexive being an atom in the lattice of coreflexives is equivalent to its
being an atom in the lattice of relations. This justifies the omission.
.

Proof
First, that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are both proper is immediate from (
15
).

To show that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
is an atom we have to show that, for all
p
,

p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

We do this by mutual implication. First, the follows-from:

p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 7.4997pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 7.4997ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

predicate calculus
~~~\}

(p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 5.99976ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}})\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976pt(p\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 5.99976pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 5.99976ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}})

\Leftarrow

\{

left conjunct: anti-symmetry, right conjunct: Leibniz
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neq}\hskip 2.99988pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

\Leftarrow

\{

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
is proper (see above)
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

Now we prove the converse. Assume
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
. Then

p\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

anti-symmetry and assumption:
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptp

\Leftarrow

\{

assumption:
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
, so
p\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
;
(p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

Leibniz
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R

=

\{

p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\neq}\hskip 2.99988pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
(see below for proof)

R
is an atom, definition
79
(appropriately instantiated)
~~~\}

p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR

=

\{

assumption:
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}
, monotonicity
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

In order to verify the penultimate step in the above calculation, we show that
p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\Rightarrow}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{false}

under the assumption that
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
.

p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

cone rule: (
4
)
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

domains: theorem
19
(a)
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

\Rightarrow

\{

assumption:
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
, composition of coreflexives is intersection
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

assumption:
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
, cone rule: (
4
)
~~~\}

\mathsf{false}~~.

\Box

Corollary 96

If
R
is a proper atom,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are particles.

Proof
By lemma
95
and definition
82
of a point, if
R
is a proper atom,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

are points. Thus, by lemma
93
,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are particles.
\Box

We now aim to verify properties
87
(b), (c) and (d) of a pair, with
Z
instantiated to proper atom

R
. Property
87
(b) is the following lemma.

Lemma 97

A proper atom is a rectangle.

Proof
Suppose
R
is a proper atom. Then

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

domains: theorem
19
(a)
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
, cone rule: (
4
)
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

domains: (
14
)
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

by corollary
96
,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are particles;

corollary
91
(c) with
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

domains: (
14
)
~~~\}

R~~.

That is,
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR
. Thus, by definition,
R
is a rectangle.

\Box

We now have all the ingredients for our goal.

Lemma 98

Suppose
R
is a proper atom. Then, assuming axiom
75
,

R
is a pair.

Proof
Suppose
R
is a proper atom.
We have to verify properties
87
(b), (c) and (d) (with
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptR
) of a pair.

Property
87
(b) is lemma
97
.
Properties
87
(c) and (d) assert that
R
is a bijection. To prove this,
let
J
be an index of
R
. (This is where axiom
75
is assumed.) Then

J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

R
is an atom
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

J
is an index of
R
, definition
49

~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

That is,
J\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR
. But
R
is a rectangle and thus a difunction. So, applying
lemma
70
,
J
—and thus
R
— is a bijection, as required.

\Box

To conclude this section and sections
7.3
and
7.4
, we have:

Theorem 99

Assuming axiom
75
, for all types
A
and
B
, and all
relations
R
of type
A{\sim}B
,
R
is a proper atom iff
R
is a pair.

Proof
This is a combination of lemmas
88
and
98
.

\Box

7.6
Pairs of Points and the All-or-Nothing Rule

The final step is to show that we can derive the “all-or-nothing” rule. Throughout this section we
assume axiom
75
.

Lemma 100

If
Z
is a pair then
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are particles.

Proof
Suppose
Z
is a pair. We begin by showing
that its left and right domains are also pairs.

Properties
87
(a), (c) and (d) —with
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
— are properties of the domain operators .
This leaves
87
(b). For the instance
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
, we have:

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

domains: theorem
19
(a) and (b)
~~~\}

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

assumption:
Z
is a pair, so
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptZ
~~~\}

Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

assumption:
Z
is a pair, so
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

The proof that
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
is a pair is symmetrical.

It now follows immediately that
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
Z{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
are squares: a square is a symmetric rectangle, and both are
rectangles (see above); also, both are coreflexives, and coreflexives are symmetric.

\Box

The following theorem is
[
Voe99
, lemma 4.41(d)]
.

Theorem 101

For all
Z
,

\mathsf{pair}{.}Z\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976pt{\left\langle\exists\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\wedge}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{point}{.}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}~~.

Proof
By mutual implication. First,

\mathsf{pair}{.}Z

\Rightarrow

\{

lemma
100
;

definition
87
(b) and
\left[\hskip 4.49982ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Z\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt\right]
~~~\}

\mathsf{particle}\hskip 1.49994pt{.}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976pt\mathsf{particle}\hskip 1.49994pt{.}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976ptZ\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

\Rightarrow

\{

corollary
92
~~~\}

\mathsf{point}\hskip 1.49994pt{.}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976pt\mathsf{point}\hskip 1.49994pt{.}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976ptZ\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

\Rightarrow

\{

a{,}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptZ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
~~~\}

{\left\langle\exists\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\wedge}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{point}{.}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptZ\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}~~.

For the converse, assume that
a
and
b
are points. We have to prove that
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is a pair. Applying
definition
87
, this means checking four properties:

(a)

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 2.99988pt{\neq}\hskip 2.99988pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~,

(b)

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b~~,

(c)

(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~,

(d)

(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b)~~.

Properties (a) and (b) are instances of the cone rule together with the assumption that
a
and
b
are
proper. We prove (c) as follows.
