(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

converse
~~~\}

a\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptb^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pta

=

\{

assumption:
b
is a point, cone rule: (
4
)
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a

=

\{

assumption:
a
is a point; so, by corollary
93
,
a
is a pair;

definition
87
(b) with
Z\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994pta
~~~\}

a

=

\{

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is a non-empty rectangle
~~~\}

(a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Property (d) is proved symmetrically.

\Box

We conclude with the theorem that Glück’s “all-or-nothing” axiom
[
Glü17
]
is a consequence of
our axiom of choice.

Theorem 102
(All or Nothing)

{\left\langle\forall\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b{,}R\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\wedge}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{point}{.}b\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\vee}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}~~.

Proof
Suppose
a
and
b
are points. By theorem
101
,
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is a pair.
So, by lemma
88
,
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is an atom. Applying the definition of atomic, we have, for all
R
,

\mathsf{true}

=

\{

monotonicity,
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b

=

\{

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is an atom, definition
79
~~~\}

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\vee}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b~~.

\Box

The significance of the all-or-nothing rule is that, together with theorem
81
,
it follows that the lattice of relations of type
A{\sim}B
is isomorphic to the powerset
2^{A{{\times}}B}
.

Theorem 103

Suppose, for types
A
and
B
, the lattices of coreflexives of types
A
and
B

are both extensional (i.e. complete, universally distributive and saturated). Then the lattice of relations of type

A{\sim}B
is saturated; the atoms are elements of the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
where
a
and
b
are atoms of
the poset of coreflexives (of types
A
and
B
, respectively). It follows that, if the lattice of relations of type

A{\sim}B
is complete and universally distributive, it is isomorphic to the powerset of the set of elements of
the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
where
a
and
b
are points of types
A
and
B
, respectively.

Proof
By theorems
101
and
99
,

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is an atom. It suffices to prove that the lattice of relations of type
A{\sim}B
is saturated.
This is easy: for all
R
of type
A{\sim}B
,

R

=

\{

\mathbb{I}
is unit of composition,

lattices of coreflexives of types
A
and
B
are extensional
~~~\}

{\left\langle{\cup}a\hskip 1.49994pt{:}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{:}\hskip 1.49994pta\right\rangle}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\left\langle{\cup}b\hskip 1.49994pt{:}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{point}{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{:}\hskip 1.49994ptb\right\rangle}

=

\{

distributivity of composition over

~~~\}

{\left\langle{\cup}\hskip 1.49994pta{,}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\wedge}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{point}{.}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}

=

\{

all-or-nothing rule: theorem
102
,
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
is zero of supremum
~~~\}

{\left\langle{\cup}\hskip 1.49994pta{,}b\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pt\mathsf{point}{.}a\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathsf{point}{.}b\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}~~.

That the lattice of relations is a powerset follows from theorem
81
. By theorem

101
, every pair is a relation of the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
; also, by lemma
88
,

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
is an atom.

\Box

Summarising theorem
103
, the
saturation
property is that

(104)

{\left\langle\forall{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{:}{:}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}\right\rangle}~~.

Combining theorem
103
with theorem
81
, we get the
irreducibility

property: if
\mathcal{R}
is a function with range relations of type
A{\sim}B
and source
K
, then, for all points
a
and
b
of
appropriate type,

(105)

a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt{\cup}\mathcal{R}\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997pt{\left\langle\exists{}k\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptk{\in}K\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathcal{R}{.}k\right\rangle}~~.

Property (
104
) formalises the interpretation of the property
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
as the
property
(a,b){\in}R
in standard set-theoretic accounts of relation algebra.

Theorem
103
assumes that the lattices of coreflexives (of appropriate type) are extensional.
Conversely, if we assume that, for all types
A
and
B
, the lattice of relations of type
A{\sim}B
is extensional then
so is the lattice of coreflexives of type
A
, for all
A
. This is theorem
106
.
(The proof of theorem
106
can be found in the companion document
[
BV22
]
.)

Theorem 106

Suppose, for all types
A
and
B
, the lattice of relations of type

A{\sim}B
is extensional, whereby the atoms are elements of the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
where
a
and
b
are atoms of
the poset of coreflexives (of types
A
and
B
, respectively). Then, for all
A
, the lattice of coreflexives of type
A

is extensional.

\Box

Combining theorems
103
and
106
, we get:

Corollary 107

Suppose, for all types
A
and
B
, the lattice of relations of type
A{\sim}B
is
complete and universally distributive.
Then for all types
A
and
B
, the lattice of relations of type
A{\sim}B
is extensional
iff for all types
A
, the lattice of coreflexives of type
A
is extensional.

\Box

Although the saturation property allows us to identify atoms of the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
with
elements
(a,b)
of the set
A{{\times}}B
, it does not establish that the operators of relation algebra (converse,
composition etc.) correspond to their standard set-theoretic interpretations. This is straightforward.
For example, for composition we have, for all
R
and
S
,

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S

=

\{

saturation: (
104
)
~~~\}

{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\right\rangle}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}b^{\prime}{,}c\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptb^{\prime}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptb^{\prime}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\right\rangle}

=

\{

distributivity
~~~\}

{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b{,}b^{\prime}{,}c\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988ptb^{\prime}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b^{\prime}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\right\rangle}

=

\{

b
and
b^{\prime}
are points, so
b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\equiv}\hskip 2.99988ptb^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptb

case analysis on
b^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptb\hskip 4.49982pt{\vee}\hskip 4.49982ptb^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994ptb
, one-point rule
~~~\}

{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b{,}c\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptb{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptS\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\right\rangle}

=

\{

b
ranges over points, so
b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
, cone rule: (
4
)
~~~\}

{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b{,}c\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptb{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptS\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\right\rangle}

=

\{

range disjunction
~~~\}

{\left\langle\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}c\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pt{\left\langle\exists{}b\hskip 4.49982pt{:}{:}\hskip 4.49982pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\wedge}\hskip 2.99988ptb{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptS\right\rangle}\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\right\rangle}~~.

Comparing the first and last lines of this calculation (and interpreting
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
as
(a,b){\in}R
and

b{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}c\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptS
as
(b,c){\in}S
) we recognise the standard set-theoretic definition of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
.

The important step to note in the above calculation is the use of the distributivity of composition
over union. The validity of such universal distributivity — both from the left and from the right—
is a consequence of the Galois connections (
5
) and (
6
) defining factors. A similar step needed
in the calculation for converse relies on the fact that converse is the upper and lower adjoint of itself.

We conclude this section with a brief comparison of extensionality as formulated here with the
notion of extensionality formulated by Voermans
[
Voe99
]
.

Voermans
[
Voe99
, section 4.5]
postulated that the lattice of binary relations of a given type is saturated
by relations of the form
X{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}Y
where
X
and
Y
are
particles
. Relations of this form are then shown to
model pairs
(x,y)
in standard set-theoretic presentations of relation algebra. Here, we have postulated
that each type
A
forms a lattice that is saturated by
points
: see axiom
86
; this postulate
is combined with our axiom of choice: all pers have an index. Then pairs in standard set-theoretic
presentations of relation algebra are modelled by relations of the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
, where
a
and
b
are points.
Because particles are points (corollary
92
), the saturation property postulated by
Voermans is formally stronger than axiom
86
. As a consequence, it becomes slightly
harder to establish that, for example, the composition of two relations does indeed correspond to the
set-theoretic notion of composition. (See
[
Voe99
, section 4.5]
for details of what is involved.) More
importantly, the
combination of axioms
75
and
86
facilitates a better separation of
concerns: axiom
75
provides a powerful extension of point-free reasoning, whilst axiom

86
fills the gap where pointwise reasoning is unavoidable.

8
Independence of the Axioms

In this paper, we have considered various additions to the basic axioms of point-free relation algebra: the
cone rule, our axiom of choice, extensionality and the all-or-nothing rule. The question arises to what
extent these additional rules are independent of one another.

Elementary examples of point-free relation algebra give some insight. In all the examples, there is just
one, unnamed, type. The 1-element algebra
has just one element: in this algebra
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. The 2-element algebra has two elements:
in this algebra
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. There is a 3-element algebra that
has distinct elements
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
,
\mathbb{I}
and
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. (There is also a 3-element algebra in which
\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
.)

The cone rule is independent of the other rules since, given any two algebras, one can define their
product in such a way that the cone rule is not satisfied but the validity of other rules is preserved. See

[
Voe99
, section 3.4.3]
for more details.

The 1-element algebra is the algebra of concrete relations on the empty set and
the 2-element algebra is the algebra of concrete relations on a set with exactly one element. Both these
algebras satisfy all four additional axioms.

The 1-element and 2-element algebras have the property that
\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. So, for any
R
,

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR
. In such algebras, our axiom of choice is valid (because every element is an index of
itself); extensionality is also valid but, if there
are more than two elements, the cone rule is not valid. (If the algebra is complemented, it is a Boolean
algebra.)

The 3-element algebra does not satisfy our axiom of choice since
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
does not have an index. Nor does
it satisfy the all-or-nothing rule. It does, however, satisfy the cone rule as well as being extensional.

A slightly more complicated example is needed to show that the all-or-nothing rule does not imply
our axiom of choice. Consider the algebra with a single point
a
in addition to the three constants

{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
,
\mathbb{I}
and
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. That is,
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. Add the requirement that
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
. Then
a\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
and the
all-or-nothing rule is valid. However,
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
does not have an index: the only two possibilities are
a
and
\mathbb{I}
, and
it is easily checked that neither satisfies the requirements.

The above example does not satisfy extensionality (because there is exactly one point different from
\mathbb{I}
);
nor does it satisfy the cone rule (because
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pta
). Adding the cone rule as a requirement
has the implication that
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
,
a
and
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
must be different — with the knock-on effect that several
additional elements must also be added to the algebra. The resulting
algebra, which was constructed by Jules Desharnais [private
communication] using
Mace4

[
McC10
]
, is the minimal algebra with elements
a
and
E
, in addition to the
constants
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
,
\mathbb{I}
and
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
, such that

{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathbb{I}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptE\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pta{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}E\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pta\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptE{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a\mbox{~~,}

and the cone rule and the all-or-nothing rule are both satisfied. The lattice structure of the algebra is
shown in fig.
3
.

\top\kern-6.02773pt\top
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathbb{I}}\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994ptE
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
E\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathbb{I}}
E
{\mathbb{I}}\hskip 1.49994pt\cup\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
\mathbb{I}
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}a
a
\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot

Figure 3:
All-or-Nothing and Cone Rule but not Axiom of Choice

Note that the original 4-element algebra forms the “stem” of the algebra —
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
has been
renamed
E
. Adding the cone rule causes the algebra to “blossom out” into a much larger structure.
The algebra does not satisfy our axiom of choice, because
E
does not have an index.

The combination of the all-or-nothing rule, the cone rule and extensionality implies that the lattice
of relations is isomorphic to the powerset of the set of elements of the form
a{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
where
a
and
b
are
points
[
BDGv22
, theorem 57]
. (We have already seen two examples of the theorem:
the 1-element and the 2-element algebras.)
In turn, this implies that, in standard set theory, our
axiom of choice is valid. In constructive set theory, the axiom might be deemed to be inadmissible — in the
same way that the universal axiom of choice might be deemed to be inadmissible. (Inadmissible is
different from invalid. The law of the excluded middle is inadmissible in constructive logic but is not
invalid; in constructive logic, particular instances of excluded middle must be proven by exhibiting
witnesses, but current wisdom is that a counterexample to excluded middle will never be found.)

The final question we ask is whether, in point-free relation algebra,
the universal axiom of choice is implied by our axiom of choice. Formally, the universal axiom of choice is that, for any relation
R
, there is a relation
f
such that

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,
f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathbb{I}
and
f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
. (In words,
f
is a functional approximation to
R
with the same right domain
as
R
; for given point
b
,
f{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}b
“chooses” a point
a
in the left domain of
R
such that
a
and
b
are related by
R
.)

The 3-element algebra satisfies the universal axiom of choice but not our axiom of
choice. So the universal axiom of choice does not imply our axiom of choice. It is an open question
whether or not our axiom of choice implies the universal axiom of choice.

9
Conclusion

Point-free relation algebra has been developed over many, many years (beginning in the 19th century)
and is generally regarded as a much better basis for the development of the theory of relations than
pointwise reasoning. However, for practical applications, pointwise reasoning is at times unavoidable.
For example, path-finding algorithms on graphs must ultimately be expressed in terms of the nodes
and edges of the graph (the points and elements of the relation defined by the graph). Good practice is to
develop such algorithms in a stepwise fashion, beginning with point-free reasoning (typically using
regular algebra) and delaying the introduction of points until absolutely necessary.

It is common practice to represent an equivalence relation by choosing a specific element of each
equivalence class. For example, the class of integers modulo
3
is commonly represented by the set of
three elements
0
,
1
and
2
. The characterisation of an equivalence relation by a representative function is
not derivable in point-free relation algebra since there is a constructive element in the choice of
representatives. Extensions to point-free relation algebra, such as the postulate that relations form a
so-called “power allegory”
[
Fv90
, 2.4]
, are intended to enable pointwise reasoning but nevertheless
fail to properly capture the use of representatives. Our axiom of choice (axiom
75
)
together with our point-free formulation of the notion of an index of a relation does capture the use of
representatives. The
strength of the axiom together with the fact that an index of a relation has the same type as the relation
makes the notion of an index —in our view— very attractive and useful.
Moreover, its combination with the extensionality axiom (axiom
86
)
permits the derivation of Glück’s “all-or-nothing” axiom
[
Glü17
]
. In this way, point-free
reasoning has been strengthened whilst also facilitating pointwise reasoning when unavoidable.

It might be argued that our axiom of choice is too strong. On the contrary, we would argue that it
corresponds much better to standard practice. For example, the computation of the strongly
connected components of a graph involves computing a representative node for each component.
(Tarjan
[
Tar72
]
and Sharir
[
Sha81
]
call the representative of a strongly connected
component of a graph the “root” of the component; Cormen, Leiserson and Rivest
[
CLR90
, p.490]
call
it the “forefather” of the component.) A suggestion for future work is to exploit our notion of an index in
order to reformulate —much more succinctly— the properties of depth-first search that underlie its
effectiveness in such computations.

Our focus in this paper has been on documenting the properties of indexes and the consequences for
axiom systems enabling pointwise reasoning. The original motivation for this work was, however, quite
different. Seventy years ago, in a series of publications
[
Rig48
,
Rig50
,
Rig51
]
, Jacques Riguet
introduced the notions of a “relation difonctionelle”, the “différence” of a relation and “relations de
Ferrers”. In view of possible practical implications, particularly in respect of relational databases, our
goal was to bring Riguet’s work up to date, making it more accessible to modern audiences. In the
process, we began to realise that substantial improvements could be made by introducing the notions of
“core” and “index” of a relation, drawing inspiration from Voerman’s
[
Voe99
]
notion of the (left- and
right-) per domains of a relation. Results on cores and indexes relevant to Riguet’s work, in particular
practical applications of his notion of the “différence” of a relation (which we rename the “diagonal” of a
relation), are documented in
[
BV23
]
. Further insight into the nature of cores and indices is
documented in
[
VDB
]
where we introduce the “
thins
” ordering on relations; among the theorems
we prove is that an index of a relation is minimal with respect to the
thins
ordering. Proofs we have
omitted here are documented in the companion working document
[
BV22
]
.

Appendix. Notational Conventions

The predence rules we assume are that unary operators take precedence over all binary operators;
composition takes precedence over union and intersection,
which have equal precedence; next comes the subset ordering and finally the logical operators, which
follow the usual precedence rules except that disjunction and conjuction have the same precedence. We
endeavour to add spacing to suggest the precedence.

Our calculations consist of a sequence of terms, successive terms being
separated by a relation symbol and a “hint” indicating why the relation holds between the terms.
The relations between terms are always ordering relations and, typically but not always, the conclusion
of the calculation is the relation between the initial and final terms (which is implicitly deduced by
transitivity properties of the connecting relations). We
endeavour to use equality steps whenever possible, even in the case that the final conclusion is an
ordering. (This is because an equality step is safe in the sense that no loss of information is incurred when
making the step. Calculations that reach a dead end often do so because an ordering is introduced that is
too strong.) Sometimes an equality is established by exploiting the
anti-symmetry of an ordering relation. In such cases, the initial and final terms are identical and the
conclusion is that all terms in the calculation are equal. Sometimes we announce the intention to exploit
anti-symmetry by the use of the word “mutual” (as in, for example, “mutual implication” or “mutual
inclusion”).

The first rule of logic is the rule sometimes known as “substitution of equals for equals”: two things are
equal exactly when one can be substituted for the other in any context. The rule is
generally attributed to Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz. The use of the rule is often implicit in our calculations
but occasionally we refer to it by the hint “Leibniz”. The centrality of the rule is reinforced by the
ubiquitous overloading of Robert Recorde’s “
{=}
” symbol to denote equality. So the symbol is used for
equality of numbers, sets, functions, etc. In some circumstances,
however, the overloading can be confusing. A simple example is that the boolean
x\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt0
is equal to the
boolean
x{+}1\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt1
, whatever the value of
x
. However, it could be confusing to overload Robert Recorde’s equality
symbol to express their equality as in

(x\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt0)\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(x{+}1\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt1)~~.

For this reason, it is convenient to introduce a special symbol “
{\equiv}
” which we sometimes use to denote
the equality of booleans; this symbol is given a lower precedence than Robert Recorde’s symbol, as
exemplified by

x\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt0\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982ptx{+}1\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt1~~.

A more important reason for introducing an additional symbol is that boolean equality is associative. In a
continued equality
a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptc
(whatever the type of
a
,
b
and
c
), the transitivity of equality is implicitly
assumed. For booleans
a
,
b
and
c
,
a\hskip 1.49994pt{\equiv}\hskip 1.49994ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\equiv}\hskip 1.49994ptc
has a different meaning: its value is determined by
evaluating
(a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptb)\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptc
or
a\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(b\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptc)
; both yield the same result. We don’t exploit the associativity of
boolean equality in this paper. We do exploit its transitivity as well as Leibniz’s rule frequently in our
calculations. Consequently, we most commonly use Robert Recorde’s symbol to denote the equality of
boolean expressions (as well as expressions of other types).
